Title: Positive Bidiagonal Factorizations and Mixed-Type Chebyshev Multiple Orthogonal Polynomials

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08568v1

Published Time: Wed, 07 Oct 2026 01:59:28 GMT

Number of Pages: 56

Markdown Content:
POSITIVE BIDIAGONAL FACTORIZATIONS AND MIXED-TYPE CHEBYSHEV MULTIPLE ORTHOGONAL POLYNOMIALS 

MANUEL MAÑAS Abstract. We characterize when a banded totally positive matrix admits a positive bidiagonal factorization. In finite dimension such a factorization always exists. For semi-infinite matrices of arbitrary finite bandwidth, existence is equivalent to convergence of a finite family of positive series determined by the Metelmann factors. Their reciprocals are the limiting values of scalar Jacobi continued fractions. We also prove that every bounded banded totally positive matrix can be approximated in operator norm by matrices admitting positive bidiagonal factorizations, through perturbations confined to a fixed initial block. For banded Toeplitz matrices, the negative roots of the polynomial symbol give a global factorization with bounded factors. Ordered partitions of these roots determine explicit mixed-type multiple orthogonal polynomials. Applying the existing spectral Favard theorem, we compute their matrix of positive measures: its support is one interval, and the measure is purely absolutely continuous with an algebraic density of rank one. These formulas also give transition and first-return laws for the associated killed random walks.

1. Introduction A banded matrix has only finitely many nonzero diagonals. Two ways of expressing its positivity are useful. Banded total positivity (BTP) requires every minor not forced to vanish by the band pattern to be positive. A positive bidiagonal factorization (PBF) expresses the matrix as a product of unit lower bidiagonal factors, a positive diagonal, and unit upper bidiagonal factors, with positive entries on every 

Department of Theoretical Physics, Faculty of Physical Sciences, Complutense University of Madrid, 28040 Madrid, Spain 

E-mail address : manuel.manas@ucm.es .2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 15B48; Secondary 42C05, 47B35, 33C45, 60J10. 

Key words and phrases. Banded total positivity; positive bidiagonal factorization; Toeplitz matrix; mixed-type multiple orthogonality; Chebyshev polynomials; matrix of measures; killed random walk.  

> 1arXiv:2610.08568v1 [math.CA] 6 Oct 2026 2POSITIVE BIDIAGONAL FACTORIZATIONS AND MIXED-TYPE CHEBYSHEV POLYNOMIALS

allowed off-diagonal. The first condition is stated in terms of determinants; the second gives factors from which the matrix can be constructed. The first objective is to determine when these two conditions agree. They agree at every fixed finite size, but a semi-infinite matrix can have positive factorizations of all finite leading sections without having one choice of factors valid for every row. We characterize global existence for every pair of finite bandwidths: all the specified limits of ratios of triangular inverse minors must be positive. The ratios are convergents of scalar Jacobi continued fractions, and their limits are the reciprocals of adjacent Metelmann product series. Thus global existence is equivalent to convergence of those series. When the conditions fail, changing only a fixed initial block still suffices to approximate any bounded BTP matrix by matrices with a PBF. The second objective is to turn the factorization into explicit polynomial and measure formulas for Toeplitz matrices, whose entries are constant along each diagonal. As an application, we use those formulas to compute transition and return probabilities for associated random walks. For Toeplitz matrices, the classical negative-root criterion supplies an upper–lower product. The constructive step needed here is to convert that product into a global lower–diagonal–upper PBF, with bounded factors and allowed positive entries bounded away from zero. Adjacent bidiagonal exchanges achieve this without changing the matrix. The distinction between finite and global factorization is essential: our counterexample differs from a Toeplitz matrix only in its initial 2 × 2 block, yet has no global PBF. Thus the Toeplitz tail alone does not guarantee the factorization. The spectral Favard theory for matrices with a PBF [8, 11] provides the link between these objectives. For the bounded Toeplitz matrices considered here, it supplies compactly supported positive measures for the polynomial solutions of the left and right recurrences with compatible initial conditions. Here “mixed type” means that vector polynomials on both sides enter the orthogonality relations. The measures form a rectangular array; positivity is required of each scalar measure. The factorization fixes their normalization, and integrating a power of the variable between the left and right polynomials recovers an entry of the corresponding matrix power. Our Toeplitz formulas make this representation explicit. We use the name mixed-type Chebyshev polynomials for the vector polynomial systems constructed from these constant-coefficient band recurrences and their PBF initial conditions. The name refers to the classical scalar specialization: for the normalized binomial symbol (1 + 𝑧 )2, 𝑝 = 𝑞 = 1 and both polynomial families equal 𝑈 𝑛 (( 𝑥 − 2)/ 2), where 𝑈 𝑛 is the Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind. More generally, the scalar case is an affine change of variable and reciprocal rescalings of 𝑈 𝑛 on the two sides; the exact formulas are (3.25)–(3.26). Higher bandwidths replace this scalar recurrence by paired vector recurrences and scalar orthogonality by mixed-type orthogonality. The principal results are as follows. (1) Finite equivalence. A finite matrix is BTP if and only if it admits a PBF (Theorem 2.6). Thus the two conditions characterize the same class at each fixed size and bandwidth. The proof preserves the matrix and its normalized lower–diagonal–upper (LDU) decomposition. It varies only the decomposition of the lower and upper triangular factors. Proposition 2.7 describes all ordered positive factorizations by a bounded open set of initial parameters, whose allowed values are determined by finitely many polynomial inequalities. Propositions 2.9 and 2.15 give the successive linear recurrences and strictly decreasing necessary bounds on an initial parameter, while Corollary 2.10 gives the finite branched continued fractions. (2) A complete semi-infinite existence criterion. Theorem 2.25 and Corollary 2.26 characterize global PBF by 𝑝 + 𝑞 − 2 positive limits of decreasing ratios of inverse minors. Proposition 2.23 identifies the ratios with the convergents of scalar Jacobi continued fractions, one for each adjacent pair of Metelmann factors. Their limits are the reciprocals of the adjacent Metelmann product series. Convergence of those series supplies uniform bounds that make a single choice of positive initial parameters valid for all rows. The criterion applies to variable coefficients and arbitrary finite bandwidths; Remark 2.27 states the additional conditions for bounded factors. Proposition 2.29 classifies the case of eventually constant Metelmann parameters, and Remark 2.30 separates the three obstructions already present in lower bandwidth four. (3) Semi-infinite obstruction and finite-rank approximation. Theorem 2.17 gives an explicit bounded example for which every finite section has a PBF but no global PBF exists. Corollary 2.19 gives a POSITIVE BIDIAGONAL FACTORIZATIONS AND MIXED-TYPE CHEBYSHEV POLYNOMIALS 3

general inverse-entry obstruction, with examples in every lower bandwidth. Theorem 2.31 proves that every bounded semi-infinite BTP matrix is a norm limit of bounded matrices with PBF. The perturbations have rank at most 𝑝 + 𝑞 − 2 and are confined to the initial ( 𝑝 + 𝑞 − 1) × ( 𝑝 + 𝑞 − 1)

block. Proposition 2.33 describes their effect on the essential spectrum and the initial square block of the resolvent. (4) The general Toeplitz case. For fixed positive extreme coefficients, the negative-root condition for the polynomial symbol is equivalent to total nonnegativity (TN), to BTP, and to the existence of a PBF (Theorem 2.36). Repeated negative roots are allowed. (5) Explicit mixed-type polynomials and their measure. Splitting the 𝑝 + 𝑞 negative roots of the Toeplitz symbol into two ordered groups of sizes 𝑝 and 𝑞 determines two families of vector polynomials. We give their generating functions and coefficients (Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.3). They are biorthogonal for a 𝑞 × 𝑝 matrix of positive measures, whose support is one explicit interval and whose density is algebraic and has rank one (Theorems 3.8 and 3.9). (6) Coincident and geometrically spaced roots. For the normalized repeated-root symbol (1 + 𝑧 ) 𝑝 +𝑞 ,the Toeplitz entries are binomial coefficients. The associated polynomials have finite-sum expressions, and the measure has an explicit trigonometric parametrization, including the powers and leading constants describing each density entry near the origin (Theorem 3.19). Geometrically spaced roots give a 𝜏 -deformation: ordinary binomial coefficients are replaced by Gaussian binomial coefficients, while the PBF and polynomial generating functions remain explicit (Proposition 3.22). (7) A probabilistic realization. Section 4 identifies the negative-root Toeplitz class with killed Poisson-binomial walks, gives spectral transition and first-return formulas, and constructs a conservative harmonic transform of the centered walk. Metelmann’s band factorization [27], its BTP characterization, and the implication PBF ⇒ BTP [9, Theorem 2.16 and Proposition 2.21] are the starting points. The finite result here establishes the converse by replacing the initial zeros of the Metelmann factors while preserving the matrix. The TN factorization and approximation results of de Boor and Pinkus [14] are classical antecedents. The polynomial-root criterion in the Toeplitz theorem is the classical Aissen–Schoenberg–Whitney theorem [1]. Our proof makes its connection with a lower–diagonal–upper PBF explicit in arbitrary bandwidth. The tetradiagonal case, including its continued-fraction analysis and Toeplitz examples, was studied in [7]. Proposition 2.23 recovers that scalar fraction at lower bandwidth two and gives a family of Jacobi fractions for arbitrary bandwidths. For the BTP class, positivity of all their limits is the complete global existence criterion. The proof uses inverse-minor identities and a uniform perturbation argument. There are classical antecedents for writing a differential or difference operator as a product of first-order operators with positive weights. Trench characterized a canonical differential factorization by divergence of integrals at an endpoint [30]; Hartman developed determinant methods for difference equations [21]. Harris, Krueger, and Trench treated difference operators of arbitrary order: their theorem starts from a positive factorization and replaces it by one with divergent interior sums [20, Theorem 3]. Factorizations on more general domains, including continuous and discrete ones, are studied in [16]. Our question is whether a prescribed Metelmann product can be refactored with every allowed entry positive, while retaining the whole matrix, including its initial rows. The series 𝔖 𝑘 of Subsection 2.4 are computed from that prescribed product, whose initial zeros encode the left boundary. Their convergence characterizes the required refactorization. The convergence and divergence tests just mentioned concern different factorizations and different requirements. Preserving the initial rows is essential: the matrix 𝑇 ∗ in Theorem 2.17 admits a PBF on every finite leading section but has no PBF valid in all rows simultaneously. Triangular systems handle finite refactorization, and bidiagonal exchanges give the explicit Toeplitz construction. The combinatorial and spectral theory in [25] starts from a prescribed PBF and develops cyclic refactorizations, sparse recurrences, and continued fractions for projected resolvents. We use its positive upper–lower exchange in a normalization allowing a variable superdiagonal (Lemma 2.35). The existence criterion here starts from a given BTP matrix and decides whether any global PBF exists. Proposition 2.9 and Corollary 2.10 address a further constructive question: they recover the factor entries from free initial parameters through recurrences of decreasing orders and finite branched continued fractions. 4 POSITIVE BIDIAGONAL FACTORIZATIONS AND MIXED-TYPE CHEBYSHEV POLYNOMIALS 

The ( 𝑝, 𝑞 ) = (2, 1) binomial recurrence recovers, after scaling and diagonal similarity, the uniform multiple-orthogonality system of [6]. More generally, for every 𝑝 = 𝑟 , 𝑞 = 1, the binomial recurrence is, after scaling and diagonal similarity, the constant-coefficient hypergeometric system in [23, Theorem 8.5]; that work also gives its polynomial and Meijer– 𝐺 measure formulas and its bidiagonal production-matrix factorization. Transposition covers 𝑝 = 1, 𝑞 = 𝑟 . The present construction provides a common rectangular mixed-type normalization for arbitrary positive bandwidths 𝑝, 𝑞 . Its probabilistic formulas specialize the general formulas for Markov chains with PBF in [26]. Stochastic bidiagonal factorizations and their spectral representation are also developed in [12]. The measure-existence theory for matrices with a global PBF was established in the bounded case in [8] and extended to unbounded matrices in [11]. We use the version where 𝑇 itself admits a PBF. The criterion in Section 2 decides when a given BTP matrix has such a factorization. For the spectral application in Section 3, the Toeplitz matrices are bounded, so the existing Favard theorem gives positive measures with compact support and biorthogonality; compact support also gives uniqueness from the moments. We compute the polynomials, algebraic boundary values, support, and endpoint behavior explicitly. In particular, the factorized representation of the matrix of measures discussed in [8, Remark 9.7] is obtained here for the Toeplitz class of Theorem 3.9. Constant high-order recurrences and their Nikishin-type measures have an established literature [4, 15]; our normalization is the rectangular mixed-type ( 𝑝, 𝑞 ) one supplied by a PBF. The support argument uses the classical limiting-spectrum theorems of Schmidt–Spitzer and Ullman [29, 31]. Moment-matrix constructions are treated in [10]. Gaussian 𝑞 -binomial band matrices and their LU decomposition were studied in [2]. Here the geometric-root family is treated through its individual bidiagonal factors, mixed-type polynomial normalization, and matrix measure. 2. Banded total positivity and bidiagonal factorizations We first recall the banded total positivity criterion and the Metelmann factorization to which it is equivalent. We then refactor each finite matrix so that every allowed bidiagonal entry is positive and the matrix is preserved. An explicit semi-infinite example gives positive parameter intervals whose intersection is empty. We prove a complete criterion for global factorization through limits of inverse minors and convergent series of Metelmann products. We also approximate every bounded semi-infinite BTP matrix by matrices with PBF through perturbations confined to a fixed initial block. The finite-rank resolvent formula records the spectral effect of the perturbation. We conclude with the Toeplitz case, where a root criterion yields a global PBF. The parameter recurrences and inverse-entry bounds explain why finite factorizations need not extend to all rows. The approximation theorem then shows how small changes at the boundary restore a global factorization. These results address the general banded problem; the Toeplitz theorem supplies the exact construction used in the subsequent polynomial and probabilistic applications. 2.1. Positivity and Metelmann factors. We fix the positivity and bandwidth conventions needed throughout the paper. The Metelmann factorization stated below is the starting point for the finite refactorization argument; its initial zero entries are distinguished from the positive entries of its nonidentity bidiagonal blocks. Throughout, positive and nonnegative scalar quantities mean > 0 and ≥ 0, respectively. We write 

N = {1, 2, . . . } and N0 = {0, 1, 2, . . . }. Matrix entries are indexed from zero. An integer index set 

{𝑎, . . . , 𝑏 } is empty when 𝑏 < 𝑎 . A matrix of lower bandwidth 𝑝 and upper bandwidth 𝑞 satisfies 

𝑇 𝑖, 𝑗 = 0 outside −𝑞 ≤ 𝑖 − 𝑗 ≤ 𝑝 . For an increasing row list (𝑖 1, . . . , 𝑖 𝑟 ) and column list ( 𝑗 1, . . . , 𝑗 𝑟 ), its minor is structurally admissible if 

−𝑞 ≤ 𝑖 𝑘 − 𝑗 𝑘 ≤ 𝑝 (𝑘 ∈ { 1, . . . , 𝑟 }) .

Otherwise the band structure forces the determinant to vanish. The matrix is banded totally positive 

(BTP) when every admissible minor is positive; it is totally nonnegative (TN) when all minors are nonnegative. For a semi-infinite matrix these conditions concern all finite minors, or equivalently all finite leading sections, with the inherited band. These positive weights need not be transition probabilities. A positive bidiagonal factorization (PBF) means (2.1) 𝑇 = 𝐿 1 · · · 𝐿 𝑝 𝐷 𝑈 𝑞 · · · 𝑈 1,

where every 𝐿 𝑖 is unit lower bidiagonal with positive subdiagonal, every 𝑈 𝑗 is unit upper bidiagonal with positive superdiagonal, and 𝐷 is positive diagonal. Here “unit” means that every diagonal entry of the triangular factor equals one. The expression positive bidiagonal always refers to its allowed off-diagonal entries. Products in the semi-infinite setting are well defined entrywise because there are finitely many banded factors. The notation PBF also denotes the class of matrices admitting such a factorization. The positive-minor criterion below proves the implication PBF ⇒ BTP. Figure 1 illustrates the entrywise meaning of a PBF. Read the factors from left to right: an edge from level 𝑎 to level 𝑏 has weight equal to the factor entry in row 𝑎 , column 𝑏 . Multiply weights along each path and then sum over intermediate levels. For minors, the corresponding objects are families of paths with no shared vertices, connecting the selected rows and columns in their increasing order. By the Lindström–Gessel–Viennot lemma [24, 19], each minor of a product represented in this way equals the total weight of such families. Strict positivity corresponds to the existence of at least one such family with positive weight. For a PBF, this is expressed by the following known implication, which we recall in the present notation. For finite matrices of order 𝑑 we assume 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ { 1, . . . , 𝑑 − 1}. We write BTP (𝑑 )

Proposition 2.1 (PBF implies BTP, recalled from [9]) . Let 𝐿 1, . . . , 𝐿 𝑝 be unit lower bidiagonal matrices of order 𝑑 with positive subdiagonals, let 𝑈 1, . . . , 𝑈 𝑞 be unit upper bidiagonal matrices with positive superdiagonals, and let 𝐷 be positive diagonal. For finite matrices this is [9, Proposition 2.21], a consequence of the product property in its Theorem 2.18. A leading section of a lower–diagonal–upper product is the product of the corresponding leading sections. 2.2. Refactorization with positive parameters. The initial identity blocks in (2.2) leave some subdiagonal entries equal to zero. We will replace these zeros by small positive parameters and adjust the other entries while keeping the entire lower triangular product fixed. The construction proceeds row by row: at each row the new parameters are free, and a triangular linear system determines the remaining entries. Applying the same construction to the transpose of the upper product will then give a PBF of the original matrix. After the existence theorem, an argument determining one factor at a time will identify the associated linear recurrences and their branched continued-fraction representation. 

Lemma 2.4 (Refactorization of a fixed lower product, row by row) . Let 𝑝 ∈ { 1, . . . , 𝑑 − 1} and

Theorem 2.6 (Finite BTP equals PBF) . For every 𝑑 and 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ { 1, . . . , 𝑑 − 1},

.

More precisely, every prescribed Metelmann factorization of 𝑇 is a limit of PBFs of the same matrix 𝑇 ,with convergence of each individual bidiagonal factor. Define its unit triangular factors by 

𝐿 := b𝐿 1 · · · b𝐿 𝑝 , 𝑈 := b𝑈 𝑞 · · · b𝑈 1, 𝑇 = 𝐿𝐷𝑈. 

Along the approximating PBFs, 𝐿 1 · · · 𝐿 𝑝 = 𝐿 and 𝑈 𝑞 · · · 𝑈 1 = 𝑈 , and 𝐷 is unchanged. Thus it is the normalized LDU decomposition of 𝑇 that remains fixed; only the individual bidiagonal factors of 𝐿 and 

𝑈 vary. Proof. For 𝑇 ∈ BTP, Proposition 2.2 gives (2.2). Apply Lemma 2.4 to its lower product. Apply the same lemma, with 𝑝 replaced by 𝑞 , to the transpose of the upper product, namely b𝑈 T 

> 1

· · · b𝑈 T 

> 𝑞

. The first application gives 𝐿 1 (𝒖 ) · · · 𝐿 𝑝 (𝒖 ) = 𝐿 . For the second application, write 𝑈 T = 𝑀 1 (𝜃 ) · · · 𝑀 𝑞 (𝜃 ) and set 𝑈 𝑗 (𝜃 ) = 𝑀 𝑗 (𝜃 )T. Transposing the product reverses its order, so 𝑈 𝑞 (𝜃 ) · · · 𝑈 1 (𝜃 ) = 𝑈 . Thus 

𝑇 = 𝐿 1 (𝒖 ) · · · 𝐿 𝑝 (𝒖 )𝐷𝑈 𝑞 (𝜃 ) · · · 𝑈 1 (𝜃 )

for all parameters in the neighborhoods supplied by the lemma. Choose all components of 𝒖 and 𝜃 equal to 𝜀 > 0. Since both parameter spaces are finite-dimensional, one sufficiently small 𝜀 belongs to both neighborhoods. Every allowed bidiagonal entry is then positive, while 𝐿, 𝑈, 𝐷 remain unchanged. As 

𝜀 ↓ 0, the analytic factors converge entry by entry to the prescribed Metelmann factors. If one parameter space has dimension zero, its factors are already strictly positive and remain fixed. Conversely, Proposition 2.1 applies to any PBF and makes every structurally admissible minor positive. This proves the reverse inclusion and hence the equality of the two classes. □

The total number of free parameters in this construction is 𝑝 ( 𝑝 − 1)/ 2 + 𝑞 (𝑞 − 1)/ 2. The theorem constructs factorizations near a specified Metelmann factorization. Solving the same linear system for every choice of initial entries that makes all factors positive gives the full family of ordered positive factorizations. 

Proposition 2.7 (Initial parameters for all positive factorizations) . Fix 𝑝 ∈ { 2, . . . , 𝑑 − 1} and a lower product 𝐺 = b𝐿 1 · · · b𝐿 𝑝 as in Lemma 2.4. Put 𝑠 = 𝑝 ( 𝑝 −1)/ 2 and let 𝒖 = (𝑎 𝑖 (𝑛 )) 𝑛 ∈ { 1,..., 𝑝 −1}, 𝑖 ∈ { 1,..., 𝑝 −𝑛 }

collect the initial entries that are zero in the Metelmann factors b𝐿 1, . . . , b𝐿 𝑝 . There is a nonempty bounded open set D𝐺 ⊂ ( 0, ∞) 𝑠 , described by finitely many polynomial inequalities, such that solving the triangular system (2.4) at each row gives a bijection 

𝒖 ∈ D𝐺 ↦ −→ ( 𝐿 1 (𝒖 ), . . . , 𝐿 𝑝 (𝒖 )) 

onto all ordered factorizations 𝐺 = 𝐿 1 · · · 𝐿 𝑝 by unit positive lower bidiagonal matrices. The factor entries are rational functions of 𝒖 . Conversely, 𝒖 is recovered by reading the indicated initial entries 10 POSITIVE BIDIAGONAL FACTORIZATIONS AND MIXED-TYPE CHEBYSHEV POLYNOMIALS 

of the factors. Thus the factorizations depend analytically on exactly 𝑠 independent parameters: the derivative of the displayed map has rank 𝑠 at every point of D𝐺 .Proof. Solve the triangular system (2.4), one row at a time, with positive initial entries, retaining precisely those parameters for which every solved entry is positive. At every retained point, the pivots (2.5) are products of positive earlier entries. Back substitution therefore gives rational functions with nonzero denominators. Their signs can be fixed throughout the construction. Start with positive free parameters, each represented with denominator one. Suppose all entries obtained in earlier rows have been written as quotients of polynomials, with positive numerators and denominators on the parameter set retained so far. The coefficients of the next row system are sums of products of these entries. Its pivots are products of positive earlier entries. Forming the right-hand sides and performing back substitution consequently gives each new entry as 𝑃 (𝒖 )/ 𝑅 (𝒖 ), where 𝑅 is a product of previously positive polynomial numerators and denominators and hence 𝑅 > 0. Positivity of the new entry is exactly the additional polynomial inequality 𝑃 (𝒖 ) > 0. Repeating this procedure through the finitely many rows describes D𝐺 by a single finite system of strict polynomial inequalities: positivity of the free parameters and of the successively obtained numerators. This also proves that D𝐺 is open. It is nonempty by Lemma 2.4. Every positive factorization satisfies (2.4). Its pivots are positive, so the unique solution at each row recovers it from its initial entries. Conversely, every retained parameter vector gives positive factors whose product is 𝐺 . This proves both surjectivity and injectivity. The rational functions have nonzero denominators on the set of allowed parameters and are analytic there. The 𝑠 freely chosen initial entries are among the entries of the factors themselves. The derivative of the displayed map consequently contains an identity matrix of order 𝑠 and has rank 𝑠 everywhere. Finally, the first subdiagonal equation is 𝐺 𝑛,𝑛 −1 = Í𝑝 𝑖 =1 𝑎 𝑖 (𝑛 ). Since 𝑝 ≥ 2 and every term is positive, each freely chosen initial entry satisfies 0 < 𝑎 𝑖 (𝑛 ) < 𝐺 𝑛,𝑛 −1. These finitely many bounds prove boundedness. □

For a full matrix 𝑇 = 𝐺 𝐷𝑈 , the normalized triangular factors and 𝐷 are unique, and the lower and upper refactorizations are independent. For 𝑝, 𝑞 ≥ 2, the domain is consequently D𝐺 × D𝑈 T , of dimension 𝑝 ( 𝑝 − 1)/ 2 + 𝑞 (𝑞 − 1)/ 2. A bandwidth-one side contributes a single fixed factor. 

Remark 2.8 (Closures of matrix classes and of factorizations) . For fixed size and bandwidth, Theorem 2.6 gives PBF = BTP in every finite-dimensional matrix norm. The common closure contains the zero matrix, since 𝜀𝑇 → 0 as 𝜀 ↓ 0 for fixed 𝑇 ∈ BTP. For a fixed 𝑇 , convergence of factorizations means convergence of the entries of each ordered factor, with their product equal to 𝑇 throughout. The closure of this family contains its positive factorizations and the prescribed Metelmann factorization. In the local parameter coordinates of Lemma 2.4, the Metelmann factors correspond to the origin, approached through positive parameter vectors. The triangular systems establish the existence result in Theorem 2.6. Determining the factors one at a time also gives linear recurrences for auxiliary sequences and rational recurrences for the bidiagonal entries themselves.

If the given matrix is a product of 𝑟 positive bidiagonal factors, removing its first factor leaves a product of 𝑟 − 1 positive bidiagonal factors. Its outermost subdiagonal is positive, being a product of 

𝑟 − 1 positive entries. Hence the argument can be repeated until only one factor remains. At order 𝑟 

there are 𝑟 initial values of a linear recurrence, whose ratios are invariant under a common rescaling. The coefficients at each later stage are those of the remaining product, and therefore depend on the factors already determined. □

It does not by itself establish convergence of an infinite matrix continued fraction. The finite branched fractions of Corollary 2.10 reconstruct factors from chosen initial entries. The scalar Jacobi fractions in Proposition 2.23 below have coefficients fixed by the Metelmann factorization; their limits decide whether any global positive factorization exists.

2.3. Semi-infinite factorizations and their obstructions. Global positive parameters must belong to the admissible domains of all finite leading sections. For the following bounded tetradiagonal matrix, these domains are explicit intervals with empty intersection. Compatibility of the Metelmann factors across matrix sizes provides the starting point for the complete criterion in Subsection 2.4 and the norm approximation in Subsection 2.5. The admissible intervals (0, 1/𝑁 ) have empty intersection, and their common limiting endpoint 𝑡 = 0recovers the Metelmann factorization with 𝑎 1 = 0. This identifies the parameter responsible for the global obstruction. The Metelmann factors, with their prescribed initial zeros, are compatible across leading sections. This compatibility gives a general norm approximation through perturbations of a fixed initial block. 

Lemma 2.18 (Compatibility of the finite Metelmann factorizations) . Let 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ N be fixed. A semi-infinite matrix of bandwidth ( 𝑝, 𝑞 ) is BTP if and only if it has a Metelmann factorization (2.2) , with semi-infinite bidiagonal blocks and the same initial identity blocks. With the indicated factor order and unit bidiagonal normalization, this Metelmann factorization is unique. Its leading sections agree with the finite Metelmann factorizations for every order 𝑑 > max ( 𝑝, 𝑞 ).Proof. Suppose first that 𝑇 is BTP. For each 𝑑 > max ( 𝑝, 𝑞 ), apply Proposition 2.2 to its leading section of order 𝑑 . Write the resulting normalized triangular decomposition as 𝑇 (𝑑 ) = 𝐺 (𝑑 ) 𝐷 (𝑑 )𝑉 (𝑑 ) , where 

𝐺 (𝑑 ) is unit lower triangular and 𝑉 (𝑑 ) is unit upper triangular. Taking a leading section commutes with such a lower–diagonal–upper product: in entry (𝑖, 𝑗 ) the middle index is at most min (𝑖, 𝑗 ). Consequently, the leading section of a decomposition at order 𝑑 + 1 is a normalized triangular decomposition at order 𝑑 . All leading principal minors of 𝑇 are positive, so this decomposition is unique. It follows that 𝐺 (𝑑 ) , 𝐷 (𝑑 ) , 𝑉 (𝑑 ) are compatible as 𝑑 increases. The individual Metelmann factors of 𝐺 (𝑑 ) are also unique. Indeed, impose the initial zeros 𝑎 𝑖 (𝑛 ) = 0for 𝑛 ≤ 𝑝 − 𝑖 . The proof of Lemma 2.4 then recovers every other entry from (2.4). In row 𝑛 , the coefficients depend only on earlier rows and the pivots (2.5) are positive. Starting at row 1, induction therefore gives a unique solution. This recovery is independent of 𝑑 once 𝑑 > 𝑛 . Applying the same argument to (𝑉 (𝑑 ) )T proves compatibility and uniqueness for the upper factors. Each entry thus stabilizes exactly. These entries define the semi-infinite factors. Their product is well defined entrywise, since the number of banded factors is finite, and agrees with 𝑇 on every leading section. All entries required to be positive in (2.2) remain positive. Conversely, taking a leading section in the semi-infinite product gives a finite Metelmann factorization. Proposition 2.2 makes every sufficiently large leading section BTP, which proves positivity of every structurally admissible finite minor of 𝑇 . □

We now obtain a necessary and sufficient criterion in every finite bandwidth. There is one decreasing ratio of inverse minors for each order from one to one less than the bandwidth. Its limit is the reciprocal of a positive series computed from the unique Metelmann factors. The ratios are also the convergents of scalar Jacobi continued fractions, extending the tetradiagonal construction. We give the algebraic argument because it must remain valid before positivity of the extracted factors is known.

Remark 2.27 (Construction, complete parameter conditions, and boundedness) . For any positive factorization of the leading section of order 𝑝 + 1, form 𝑋 𝑟 and W𝑚,𝑛 as above. Approximation through an initial block. The criterion decides whether the original matrix has a global PBF. The following approximation changes the matrix itself: filling the initial Metelmann zeros produces positive factorizations and affects only a fixed finite block of the full matrix. For 𝑟 ∈ N, let 𝜄 𝑟 : C𝑟 → ℓ2 (N0) be the inclusion in coordinates {0, . . . , 𝑟 − 1}, and let 𝑃 𝑟 = 𝜄 𝑟 𝜄 T 

> 𝑟

be the corresponding orthogonal projection. The transpose notation is valid because 𝜄 𝑟 has real entries. 

Theorem 2.31 (Approximation by perturbing an initial block) . Let 𝑇 be a bounded semi-infinite BTP matrix of bandwidth ( 𝑝, 𝑞 ), where 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ N, and put 𝑚 0 = 𝑝 + 𝑞 − 1. There is a family of bounded matrices 𝑇 𝜀 , for 𝜀 > 0, of the same bandwidth and admitting a PBF, such that Δ𝜀 := 𝑇 𝜀 − 𝑇 satisfies 

(2.35) Δ𝜀 = 𝑃 𝑚 0 Δ𝜀 𝑃 𝑚 0 , rank Δ𝜀 ≤ 𝑝 + 𝑞 − 2.

The family is monotone entrywise: 

(2.36) 𝑇 ≤ 𝑇 𝜀 ≤ 𝑇 𝛿 (0 < 𝜀 ≤ 𝛿 ).

Moreover, 

(2.37) ∥𝑇 𝜀 − 𝑇 ∥2 ≤ 𝜀 ∥𝑇 1 − 𝑇 ∥2 (0 < 𝜀 ≤ 1).

In particular, 𝑇 𝜀 → 𝑇 in operator norm as 𝜀 ↓ 0, and every entry outside the initial 𝑚 0 × 𝑚 0 block is unchanged. Here 𝑇 𝜀 is a bounded operator on ℓ2 (N0), and its PBF is the entrywise product specified in

Support of the lower and upper changes. A term in 𝐺 𝜀 − 𝐺 contains a changed entry of some factor 

𝐿 𝑖 . If that entry is in row 𝑛 , then 𝑛 ≤ 𝑝 − 𝑖 . The 𝑖 − 1 factors to its left can increase the resulting row index by at most 𝑖 − 1, so the resulting row index is at most 𝑛 + 𝑖 − 1 ≤ 𝑝 − 1. The difference is strictly lower triangular. Its nonzero entries therefore satisfy

[𝑉 𝜀 − 𝑉 ]𝑎,𝑏 ≠ 0 =⇒ 𝑏 ∈ { 1, . . . , 𝑞 − 1}, 𝑎 ∈ { 0, . . . , 𝑏 − 1},

and a rank bound 𝑞 − 1. These differences vanish when 𝑝 = 1 or 𝑞 = 1, respectively.

Remark 2.34 (Eventual Toeplitz structure and spectral formulas) . If 𝑇 is eventually Toeplitz, the approximation leaves its entries outside the initial block, and hence its eventual Toeplitz symbol, unchanged. Its Fredholm essential spectrum is preserved, and the zeros of the determinant in Proposition 2.33 identify the eigenvalues of 𝑇 𝜀 in res (𝑇 ). Formula (2.41) transforms the 𝑚 0 × 𝑚 0 initial resolvent block. If its entries are algebraic, the transformed entries are algebraic as well. For a Toeplitz matrix, we characterize BTP by the zeros of the finite polynomial symbol and construct a global PBF from those roots. The construction first factors the matrix in upper–lower order and then exchanges adjacent bidiagonal factors. The exchange lemma below gives positive parameters defined simultaneously at every row. Proof. The root criterion. Set 𝑐 𝑟 = 𝑡 𝑟 −𝑞 , extended by zero for 𝑟 ∉ {0, . . . , 𝑝 +𝑞 }, and let 𝐶 = [𝑐 𝑖 − 𝑗 ]𝑖, 𝑗 ∈N0 .For finite increasing index lists 𝐼 and 𝐽 , entrywise comparison gives

Consequently 𝐶 is TN if and only if 𝑇 is TN. A sequence (𝑐 𝑟 ) whose Toeplitz matrix 𝐶 is TN is called a Pólya-frequency sequence. The finite-sequence Aissen–Schoenberg–Whitney theorem [1] states that a finite nonnegative sequence is of this type if and only if its generating polynomial has only real nonpositive zeros. Here that polynomial is Í𝑝 +𝑞 𝑟 =0 𝑐 𝑟 𝑧 𝑟 = 𝑃 (𝑧 ). If 𝑇 is TN, its entries and hence the 𝑐 𝑟 are nonnegative, so the theorem applies. Conversely, if all zeros of 𝑃 are negative, its positive constant term gives (2.44); expansion shows that its coefficients are nonnegative, and the same theorem makes 𝐶 TN. Since 𝑃 (0) = 𝑡 −𝑞 > 0, every nonpositive root is strictly negative, and 𝑡 𝑝 > 0 makes the degree exactly 𝑝 + 𝑞 . This proves that condition (1) holds if and only if condition (3) holds, including repeated roots.

Positive exchanges and diagonal normalization. Every factor in the first group is positive upper bidiagonal and every factor in the second is positive unit lower bidiagonal. Fix the following exchange procedure: take the lower factors in their original left-to-right order and move each across all upper factors to its left, starting with the nearest one, using Lemma 2.35. The relative order within each group is preserved. Call an upper–lower inversion a pair formed by an upper factor and a lower factor with the upper one to the left. Each exchange decreases the number of such inversions by one. Initially this number is 𝑝𝑞 ; after 𝑝𝑞 exchanges all lower factors are on the left. All parameters remain positive at every row. The resulting upper factors may have nonunit diagonal. Writing each as a positive diagonal times a unit upper bidiagonal and commuting positive diagonals to the left by diagonal conjugation gives (2.1), including the scalar 𝑡 −𝑞 in 𝐷 . This constructs the PBF in condition (4) from condition (3), using exactly 𝑝𝑞 exchanges. 

Uniform bounds and the remaining implications. All exchanged upper factors have superdiagonal 1. If 𝑢 𝑛 and ℓ𝑛 have positive lower bounds 𝑢 − , ℓ − and finite upper bounds 𝑢 +, ℓ +, then

> 𝑛,𝑛 −1

= 𝑢 𝑛 ℓ𝑛 /𝑑 𝑛 −1 follow from these same inequalities. They propagate through the finitely many exchanges, starting from the positive constants 𝛽 𝜈 . For diagonal normalization, a positive sequence bounded above and away from zero has an inverse with the same properties. Finite products of such sequences and their adjacent-entry ratios retain these bounds, so extracting and commuting the diagonal factors preserves boundedness and positive lower bounds. Finally, a PBF implies BTP by Proposition 2.1; hence condition (4) implies condition (2). A BTP matrix is TN because its admissible minors are positive and its other minors are zero, so condition (2) implies condition (1). Together with the implications already proved, this completes the equivalence. □

Remark 2.37 (Factor order and bidiagonal entries) . The product in (2.45) is in upper–lower order, where 

ΛΛ T = 𝐼 . In the reverse order, ΛTΛ = 𝐼 − 𝑒 0𝑒 T 

> 0

. The positive exchanges account for this initial-row term and give a lower–diagonal–upper PBF whose bidiagonal entries generally vary with the row index. 

Remark 2.38 (The root criterion and finite sections) . The negative-root condition characterizes simul-taneous BTP of all leading sections of a semi-infinite Toeplitz matrix. At a fixed size, Theorem 2.6 characterizes BTP by existence of a PBF. For example, 

3/2 11 3/2



is totally positive and admits a PBF, while 1 + ( 3/2)𝑧 + 𝑧 2 has nonreal conjugate roots. 3. Mixed-type Chebyshev multiple orthogonal polynomials and Toeplitz matrix measures The Toeplitz factorization provides a normalization for two families of mixed-type multiple orthogonal polynomials and their rectangular matrix measure. We first derive formulas for arbitrary negative roots, including the exact support and an algebraic rank-one density. We then develop the repeated-root binomial family in detail, from its bidiagonal factors to its endpoint behavior and examples, and finish with the geometric-root deformation. 3.1. The general Toeplitz class: polynomials and matrix measure. We now pass from existence to formulas for the whole negative-root Toeplitz class. The matrix determines the unordered roots, whereas the Favard normalization of the initial conditions also records how they were grouped and ordered in the initial upper–lower product. We keep this order when interchanging the factors to obtain the initial-condition matrices and both polynomial generating functions. We then reconstruct the matrix measure from a finite upper-left block of the resolvent, determine its exact support and rank-one density, and describe its endpoints. Here an ordered partition means a choice of which 𝑞 positive root parameters are assigned to the upper block and which 𝑝 to the lower block, together with an order within each block. Use the procedure fixed in the proof of Theorem 2.36: process the lower factors in their original left-to-right order, moving each past the nearest upper factor until it reaches the lower block. Follow these exchanges by the diagonal normalization described there. This specifies the PBF used below; no independence from other exchange procedures is needed for the formulas. We now compute the initial data before constructing the full polynomial families. Lemma 3.1 identifies the columns of 𝐻 −1 and the rows of 𝐾 −1 by identifying the sequences that satisfy the homogeneous equations for the successive partial products. Theorem 3.2 then extends those initial vectors to polynomial solutions of the two band recurrences and gives their generating functions. The initial-condition matrices are independent of the spectral parameter 𝑥 . Corollary 3.5 will make explicit how the polynomials and measure change when another PBF of the same matrix is used.

At the largest allowed 𝑘 , the complete homogeneous polynomial is strictly positive: all its variables are positive, and its value at index zero is one. The highest displayed power of 

𝑥 therefore has a nonzero coefficient in each nonzero polynomial. □

Different ordered partitions of the roots generally give different PBFs, initial-condition matrices, and polynomial bases; the bases are related by constant triangular matrices with diagonal entries equal to one. When all root parameters coincide, changing their order or partition leaves these matrices unchanged. We now identify the measure for the polynomial families just constructed. A 𝑞 × 𝑝 matrix of positive measures means an array (Ψ𝑏,𝑎 ) in which each entry is a finite nonnegative Borel measure. It does not mean a positive semidefinite matrix-valued measure; the array need not be square. We apply the spectral Favard theory for matrices with a PBF [8, 11]. The following corollary records its bounded case in our normalization. Compact support gives uniqueness from the moments; the subsequent root calculations determine the support and density explicitly. For such a measure, the Stieltjes transform is 𝑊 (𝜆 ) = ∫ (𝜆 − 𝑥 )−1 dΨ(𝑥 ), entry by entry. Where a continuous density exists, it is recovered as −𝜋 −1 Im 𝑊 (𝑥 + i0 ). Thus computing the boundary values of this transform will convert the polynomial construction into an explicit measure.

both supports makes this difference zero, entry by entry. The same argument applies to finite signed measures. □

This corollary applies to the PBF of Theorem 2.36 and to the explicit PBF of the geometric-root family below. The following change of normalization is an immediate consequence of the moment formula and compact-support uniqueness.

These identities describe how the polynomial bases and measure change when a different PBF of the same matrix is chosen. They do not classify all possible PBFs. Positivity of the transformed measure follows from the second PBF and Corollary 3.4; arbitrary constant changes of basis need not preserve entrywise positivity. The distinction between existence of measures and maximal polynomial degrees is part of the normality theory in [9]. For the present Toeplitz families, the degrees are verified directly by Corollary 3.3. The mixed relations then follow by the basis argument underlying [8, Corollary 9.2].

The measure support considered next is also the limiting set of eigenvalues of the finite Toeplitz sections. These statements concern the measure and the finite sections; they do not identify that interval with the full spectrum of the semi-infinite operator on ℓ2. For a nonsymmetric Toeplitz operator those sets can differ. The root expressions for the Stieltjes transform are continued from large |𝜆 |, where the operator resolvent formula has been proved. The Schmidt–Spitzer theorem [29] identifies Σ with the limiting set of eigenvalues of the finite Toeplitz sections. Those sections are BTP by Theorem 2.36, and Corollary 2.3 makes their eigenvalues positive and simple; thus 

Σ ⊆ [ 0, ∞) . Ullman’s theorem [31] states that this set is connected. The preceding root counts give 

Σ ⊆ [ 𝜆 − , 𝜆 +] and put both endpoints in Σ. A connected subset of the real line containing both endpoints contains the interval between them. Hence Σ = [𝜆 − , 𝜆 +].

One lies inside the circle and the other outside. No other root is on the circle at 𝑥 = 𝑥 ∗. Thus the colliding roots occupy positions 𝑘, 𝑘 + 1 in increasing modulus. They are the middle roots only when 

𝑘 = 𝑞 , which gives 𝑥 ∗ = 𝜆 −, outside the open interval under consideration. If 𝛽 𝑞 = 𝛽 𝑞 +1, there is no strict gap with 𝑘 = 𝑞 , so no negative collision can involve the middle roots at any 𝑥 > 0. This excludes the remaining real case. The two middle roots are therefore nonreal and conjugate throughout the open interval. They are simple and are the only roots on their modulus circle, so exactly 𝑞 − 1 roots have smaller modulus and 𝑝 − 1 have larger modulus. The sums in (3.18) are symmetric within each separated root group. They therefore continue analytically off Σ even when roots within one group coincide, and their values are real on the real complement of Σ. Applying Stieltjes inversion to (3.19) gives (3.20). This establishes the support bound without using the density formula. The next theorem determines the density and proves equality of the support with this interval. □

Positivity, rank, and support. Since 𝐻 is lower triangular and 𝐾 is upper triangular, both with diagonal entries equal to one, their first row and first column, respectively, select the constant complete homogeneous polynomial ℎ0 = 1. Thus the first components of 𝑓 and 𝑔 are one. The measures supplied by Favard are positive, so their density entries are nonnegative almost everywhere. Those entries are continuous in the open interval, and are consequently nonnegative at every point there. The first column of the density is 𝜌 𝑓 and its first row is 𝜌𝑔 T; since 𝜌 > 0, this proves 𝑓 , 𝑔 ≥ 0. Since 𝑓 1 = 𝑔 1 = 1, both vectors are nonzero, so the rank is exactly one. The first density entry is strictly positive on the whole open interval. Every open subinterval therefore has positive measure. Taking the closure and combining this with (3.20) proves (3.23). 

Absolute continuity on the full support. At a coincidence of roots inside one of the groups, the complete homogeneous polynomials in (3.18) are symmetric in those roots, so the formula is invariant under permutation of their individual branches. At a coincidence between the two groups, both limiting formulas remain finite. Indeed, near any finite 𝜆 0 the coefficients of 𝑄 are bounded and its leading and constant coefficients are fixed and nonzero. Its roots are therefore uniformly bounded and bounded away from zero. The finite sums in (3.18), and hence every entry of 𝑊 , remain bounded. Let [𝑎, 𝑏 ] be a closed interval avoiding the finitely many algebraic branch values. The branch of each entry of 𝑊 obtained from the upper half-plane extends analytically to a neighborhood of this interval. Thus 𝑊 (𝑥 + i𝜖 ) converges uniformly to its boundary value on [𝑎, 𝑏 ] as 𝜖 ↓ 0. The Stieltjes inversion formula on subintervals then identifies the whole restriction of the measure with the density 

−𝜋 −1 Im 𝑊 (𝑥 + i0 ); no singular part remains there. Any remaining singular measure must consequently be supported on the finite set of branch values, and can only consist of atoms. For each scalar entry and each real 𝑥 0, an atom is detected by 

Ψ𝑏,𝑎 ({ 𝑥 0}) = lim   

> 𝜖 ↓0

[− 𝜖 Im 𝑊 𝑏,𝑎 (𝑥 0 + i𝜖 )] .

Boundedness of 𝑊 makes this limit zero. The measure is therefore purely absolutely continuous on its full support. □

The binomial family: explicit polynomials and matrix measure. We specialize the general construction to coincident roots, which give binomial Toeplitz entries. In this case the individual bidiagonal factors and the positive diagonal have closed formulas. We derive the factorization, normalization of the initial conditions, and finite-sum polynomial formulas, then recover the matrix measure through an algebraic resolvent and a trigonometric parametrization of its density. The endpoint asymptotics and low-bandwidth examples complete this explicit description. Fix 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ N, and put 𝑚 = 𝑝 + 𝑞 . The binomial factorization makes the normalization of the initial conditions explicit. Remark 3.16 (Stieltjes transform and analytic continuation) . Formula (3.37) identifies the normalized upper-left block of the operator resolvent with the Stieltjes transform of Ψ for sufficiently large |𝜆 |. The compactly supported measure extends this Stieltjes transform analytically to C \ [ 0, 𝑐 ]. Its normalization is fixed by the matrix moments in (3.35). The resolvent formula becomes a density formula once its boundary values on the support are known. We locate the central conjugate pair of roots of 𝑄 and parametrize it trigonometrically. Its exchange across the interval produces the rank-one jump and hence the explicit matrix weight.

We next determine how the binomial density approaches each end of its support. At the origin, the coalescing roots produce different powers in the different matrix entries; we compute both these powers and their leading constants. The geometric-root subfamily of the preceding BTP Toeplitz class admits a second explicitly normalized PBF. Gaussian binomial identities give its bidiagonal factors, the initial-condition matrices determined by their ordered partial products, and both mixed-type generating functions. The general algebraic root formula then gives its matrix measure. The parameters 𝛼 > 0 and 𝜏 > 0 specify the scale and ratio of the root parameters. 𝑛 → ∞ , to 1 for 𝜏 ≤ 1 and to 𝜏 −𝑟 for 𝜏 > 1. The diagonal entries are finite products of such ratios or their inverses, times a positive constant. Each sequence of allowed entries therefore converges to a finite positive limit. Its finitely many initial entries are also positive, so the entire sequence is bounded above and away from zero. This proves the claimed bounded PBF. For the initial-condition matrices, the products in (3.1)–(3.2), with the present zero-based factor indices, are e𝐾 𝑖, 𝑗 = 𝑒 T

Finite non-symmetric band matrices with Gaussian 𝑞 -binomial coefficients, including determinant, inverse, and LU formulas, were studied by Arıkan and Kılıç [2]. The present formulas give a positive 

𝑝 + 𝑞 bidiagonal semi-infinite factorization, its explicit Favard normalization, two mixed-type polynomial families, and their positive rectangular matrix measure. 4. Probabilistic interpretation The Toeplitz coefficients also describe random walks built from independent Bernoulli trials. We identify their killing at the boundary, express transition and return laws through the mixed-type matrix measure, and construct a conservative transform of the centered walk. The resulting formulas connect the factorization and multiple orthogonal polynomials to the transition probabilities of these processes. 4.1. Bernoulli sums and absorption. We first turn the Toeplitz coefficients into transition probabilities and identify the mass lost at the boundary. This connects arbitrary negative-root symbols with Poisson-binomial increments and includes ordinary binomial increments when the root parameters coincide. We use row transition matrices: entry (𝑖, 𝑗 ) is the probability of moving from state 𝑖 to state 𝑗 in one step. Before absorption, the transition matrix on the nonnegative states can have row sum less than one; the missing mass is the probability of absorption, after which the walk no longer returns to the nonnegative states. At each step, 𝑚 independent Bernoulli trials are performed, and absorption is tested after all 𝑚 trials. A Poisson-binomial variable is a sum of independent Bernoulli variables whose success probabilities may differ. Karlin–McGregor formulas express transition probabilities as integrals of recurrence polynomials against spectral measures. The classical birth-and-death setting is tridiagonal; the mixed-type extension used here allows several diagonals in each direction [22, 26].

Absorption is determined by the position reached after all Bernoulli trials in each step. 4.2. Spectral transition and first-return laws. We now specialize the spectral transition and return formulas of [26, 12] to these walks. The matrix measure converts powers of the transition matrix into integrals of the mixed-type polynomials. Starting from the origin, these integrals give the return probabilities, and the renewal identity then yields the first-return generating function before absorption.

4.3. Centering and a conservative harmonic transform. Among the choices 𝑟 > 0 in (4.1), the one with zero mean increment is selected by the upper spectral endpoint. We identify that choice and use the expected position at absorption to construct a positive harmonic function. The resulting Doob transform rescales each transition by the ratio of the harmonic function at its arrival and departure states. It is conservative, meaning that every row sums to one, and has the interpretation of the centered walk conditioned to remain nonnegative. 

Proposition 4.3 (Centering and the upper spectral endpoint) . The unique value of 𝑟 for which the mean increment is zero is 𝑟 = 𝑧 + of Theorem 3.8. Its normalizing constant is 𝑎 (𝑧 +) = 𝜆 +. In the binomial case, 

𝑟 ∗ = 𝑞 𝑝 , 𝑎 (𝑟 ∗) = 𝑐 = 𝑚 𝑚

Proof. The mean increment in (4.2) is 𝑞 − F(𝑟 ), so it vanishes exactly when F(𝑟 ) = 𝑞 . The function F

is strictly increasing from 0 to 𝑚 ; there is therefore exactly one such 𝑟 , namely 𝑧 +. Equation (3.21) shows that 𝑎 ′ (𝑟 ) < 0 below 𝑧 + and 𝑎 ′ (𝑟 ) > 0 above it. Thus 𝑎 (𝑧 +) is its unique minimum, which Theorem 3.8 identifies with 𝜆 +.When 𝛽 𝜈 = 1, the equation 𝑚𝑟 /( 1 + 𝑟 ) = 𝑞 reduces to 𝑝𝑟 = 𝑞 . Hence 𝑟 ∗ = 𝑞 /𝑝 and each Bernoulli probability is 𝑟 ∗/( 1 + 𝑟 ∗) = 𝑞 /𝑚 . There are 𝑚 independent trials, so 𝑋 𝑛 has distribution Bin (𝑚, 𝑞 /𝑚 )

and Var (𝐽 𝑛 ) = Var (𝑋 𝑛 ) = 𝑚 (𝑞 /𝑚 )( 𝑝 /𝑚 ) = 𝑝𝑞 /𝑚 . Finally, substitution in 𝑎 (𝑟 ) = (1 + 𝑟 )𝑚 /𝑟 𝑞 gives

is a stochastic matrix on N0. Its 𝑛 -step probabilities are (4.3) , with 𝑟 = 𝑧 +, multiplied by 𝔥 𝑗 /𝔥 𝑖 .Proof. To prove almost sure absorption, fix an integer 𝑁 > 𝑖 and stop when the walk first leaves 

{0, . . . , 𝑁 − 1}; call this time 𝜎 𝑁 . The increment −𝑝 has positive probability. A sufficiently long block of such increments forces exit from this finite interval, from any of its states. Independence bounds the probability of surviving successive blocks by a geometric sequence, so 𝜎 𝑁 < ∞ almost surely. The centered walk is a martingale and its stopped values are bounded between −𝑝 and 𝑁 + 𝑞 − 1. Optional stopping, first at min (𝑛, 𝜎 𝑁 ) and then by bounded convergence, gives E𝑖 [𝑌 𝜎 𝑁 ] = 𝑖 .Let 𝑢 𝑁 be the probability of exit through the upper endpoint. If the walk never enters the negative half-line, it must exit each finite interval through its upper endpoint. Hence P𝑖 [𝜎 abs = ∞] ≤ 𝑢 𝑁 for every 𝑁 > 𝑖 , and letting 𝑁 → ∞ proves 𝜎 abs < ∞ almost surely. Conditioning to remain nonnegative is understood as a limit of conditioning on survival up to time 𝑁 ,as 𝑁 → ∞ ; it is not conditioning by division by the probability of eternal survival, which is zero for this centered walk. To identify the harmonic function used by this limit, let (H𝑘 )𝑘 ∈N be the depths of successive new strict minima of the centered walk started at zero: each new minimum is lower than all preceding positions, and H𝑘 is its negative. Set H0 = 0 and put

They are independent and identically distributed by the strong Markov property at the successive times of new strict minima. The increments have mean zero, bounded support and positive variance; both positive and negative jumps, as well as a zero jump, have positive probability. In particular the walk oscillates, and its strict descending ladder heights have the renewal function 𝑉 just identified. The renewal-function conditioning theorem therefore applies: for each fixed finite-time event, the conditional law given 𝜎 abs > 𝑁 converges to the law of (4.8) as

]T = 𝐻 −1 verifies the initial entries of this expression. 5. Conclusions and outlook Banded total positivity and positive bidiagonal factorization are equivalent in finite dimension. In the semi-infinite setting, global existence is characterized, for arbitrary finite bandwidths, by strict positivity of the limiting values of a finite family of scalar Jacobi continued fractions, or equivalently by convergence of the adjacent Metelmann product series. This criterion identifies the obstructions to extending finite factorizations to all rows. For bounded BTP matrices, these obstructions can nevertheless be removed by arbitrarily small perturbations confined to a fixed initial block. For banded Toeplitz matrices, the negative-root condition yields a global PBF with bounded factors. Ordered partitions of the roots then determine explicit mixed-type polynomial families and their matrix POSITIVE BIDIAGONAL FACTORIZATIONS AND MIXED-TYPE CHEBYSHEV POLYNOMIALS 55 

of positive measures. We obtain the support, the algebraic rank-one density, and its endpoint behavior. The same measures provide transition and first-return formulas for the associated killed random walks. The determinant inequalities in Remark 2.27 describe all admissible initial parameters, but there are infinitely many inequalities to check. A next question is to determine which classes of coefficients allow a finite description of this set, and how its boundary changes as a continued-fraction limit approaches zero. Another question is how the explicit measure formulas change when the diagonals approach constants without being constant. The counterexample shows why such an extension must include the initial rows as well as the limiting Toeplitz coefficients.
